⏺ I've read the repo and the task, and dug into the fixtures. Here's what I
  found.

  The repo

  A 2-hour take-home. A marketer uploaded a Q3 target list; the agent produced 1
  landing page + 3 LinkedIn ads per company, declared complete: True, and the
  shipped check agreed. The customer doesn't believe it.

  - src/repair_lab.py — TargetAccountTool (paginated loader),
  build_campaign_plan (paging loop + _collapse_page dedupe + deliverable
  fan-out), evaluate_campaign_coverage (the disputed check).
  - src/sources.py — five paging shapes the real account service has produced
  before. Anything I build has to survive all five, including admitting when the
  list can't be read completely.
  - fixtures/ — request, two lists (223 rows / 115 rows), the customer's note,
  10 trace events.
  - make demo / make test / make verify all run green on the starter. Green is
  explicitly not evidence.

  What actually went wrong

  1. The check is vacuous. evaluate_campaign_coverage only asks: do the rows
  that made it into the plan have four assets each? It never compares against
  the upload. A plan containing one row passes. It also just echoes
  plan["complete"], which build_campaign_plan hardcodes to True
  (repair_lab.py:124). It cannot fail for the reason the customer cares about.

  2. Dedupe is per-page, so the answer depends on page size. _collapse_page
  (repair_lab.py:56) dedupes within a page only. Duplicates that straddle a page
  boundary survive:

                      page_size=10   page_size=25  page_size=100
  rows campaigned            214            214            211
  deliverables               856            856            844

  Same input, three different answers, all reported complete. That's "a couple
  of companies came back twice."

  3. Null company_id collapses six distinct companies into one.
  str(row["company_id"]) turns None into the string "None", so these six all key
  to the same bucket:

  row-2401 Halverson Freight      row-2404 Northgate Tutoring
  row-2402 Pell & Sons Ironworks  row-2405 Riverbend Cold Storage
  row-2403 Cobalt Ridge Dental    row-2406 Aster Point Realty

  Five real companies are silently dropped. Second list has the same bug with ""
  (Unresolved Import A/B).

  4. Wrong brand kit, silently. Nine rows carry saved_brand_kit_id, and
  _make_deliverables lets the row override the kit the customer selected on the
  request — 36 deliverables shipped in brand-kit-2019-legacy. Nothing surfaces
  it. That's "some of the creative is not in the brand we picked."

  5. Every paging shape in sources.py breaks the loop. I ran all five through
  build_campaign_plan:

  ReplayingLoader                rows=428  complete=True   # every row
  duplicated
  StallingLoader                 RuntimeError: page budget exhausted
  CyclingLoader                  RuntimeError: page budget exhausted
  SilentlyShortLoader            rows=116  complete=True   # 107 rows never read
  TruncatedWithoutCursorLoader   RuntimeError: page budget exhausted

  The loop trusts truncated blindly, never detects a non-advancing or repeating
  cursor, and on next_cursor=None with truncated=True it does int(None or "0") →
  restarts at zero forever. This matches the traces: t-77b3 shows cursor 25
  handed back twice then worker_timeout — the customer's "rerun that never came
  back." And t-9f21 reports 25+25+17 = 67 rows read but claims source_row_count:
  209.

  How many logical companies

  I make it 209 for list 1:

  - 203 distinct non-null company_id values
  - + 6 rows with no company_id, each on its own unique domain that collides
  with nothing else — so each is resolvable as its own company, not merged, not
  dropped

  The neighbouring number is 205. Four domains carry two different company_ids
  each — northwind-energy vs northwind-energy-emea, same for sable-fitness and
  tessellate-capital, plus copperline-group/copperline-energy. Merge on domain
  and you get 205. These are exactly the customer's "entries I cannot tell apart
  well enough to say whether they are one customer or two." My position: don't 
  merge them — the upload asserts two ids, and silently merging a regional
  subsidiary is the same class of error as silently dropping one. Report 209 and
  flag those 4 as ambiguous rather than deciding for the customer.

  Note 209 is also the number the trace claimed — worth calling out as
  coincidence, not corroboration, since that run only read 67 rows.

  List 2 is 99 (97 ids + 2 unresolved rows). And this is why the two lists don't
  produce the same shape of answer: list 2 has zero ambiguous domain groups and
  zero brand-kit overrides. Its duplicates are all unambiguous same-id pairs.
  So list 1's report must carry an "ambiguous, needs a human" section that list
  2's simply won't have — a check that reports a single clean number for both is
  hiding something.

  What I'd propose next

  Per TASK.md the next artifact is ROADMAP.md, committed on its own before any 
  source or test edit. The core design question it has to settle is what
  "complete" means. My proposed definition:

  ▎ Every logical company in the upload has exactly 4 deliverables, each 
  ▎ traceable to a specific uploaded row; the count is independent of page size;
  ▎ the read is provably exhaustive (or the run declares itself incomplete and 
  ▎ says why); and every deliverable uses the requested brand kit or is 
  ▎ explicitly listed as an exception.

  Two things I want your call on before I draft it:

  1. The 4 ambiguous domain pairs — my recommendation is report-209-and-flag.
  The alternative is merge-to-205, or making the identity rule configurable.
  This is the judgment the task says I have to defend.
  2. The brand kit override — is saved_brand_kit_id a bug to remove, or a real
  feature that just needs to be surfaced as an exception? The customer says "not
  in the brand we picked," but TASK.md warns against repairing behavior you
  can't tie to the complaint. I lean toward surfacing, not removing — but
  silently overriding is clearly wrong either way.

  Want me to draft ROADMAP.md on those assumptions, or do you want to settle
  those two first?

❯ Can you tell me why there are two lists and not just one                      

⏺ Good question — and the answer is concrete, not just "so you can test twice."
  Let me check a couple of specific traps before I claim anything.

⏺ Bash(python3 -c "
      import json,collections,re…)
  ⎿  === sentinel for missing id ===
      list1: {'None'}
      list2: {"''"}
     … +19 lines (ctrl+o to expand)
⏺ Two reasons, and the second one is the real one.

  The stated reason

  TASK.md says it directly: make verify runs the same reporting against a
  second, differently shaped list "so you can tell a fix from a fit to one
  file." The deliverable asks for "the number your check reports for each list, 
  and why the two lists do not produce the same shape of answer." So it's a
  held-out set — but it's a hand-built one, and the differences are deliberate
  traps.

  The specific traps

  I diffed the structure. The second list is not just "more of the same, fewer
  rows":

  ┌────────────────────────────────┬─────────────────────┬─────────────────┐
  │                                │       list 1        │     list 2      │
  ├────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼─────────────────┤
  │ missing-id sentinel            │ None                │ ""              │
  ├────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼─────────────────┤
  │ row id prefix                  │ row-                │ srow-           │
  ├────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼─────────────────┤
  │ saved_brand_kit_id rows        │ 9                   │ 0               │
  ├────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼─────────────────┤
  │ ambiguous domain groups        │ 4                   │ 0               │
  ├────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼─────────────────┤
  │ name variants                  │ UPPERCASE, +Inc,    │ +Inc, identical │
  │                                │ identical           │  only           │
  ├────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼─────────────────┤
  │ dup groups straddling a page   │ 5 of 13             │ 1 of 15         │
  │ (size 25)                      │                     │                 │
  ├────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼─────────────────┤
  │ logical companies              │ 209                 │ 99              │
  └────────────────────────────────┴─────────────────────┴─────────────────┘

  Each row is a way to pass list 1 and be wrong:

  The sentinel flips. Write if company_id is None and list 1 goes green while
  list 2 silently re-collapses Unresolved Import A and B into one company. The
  correct predicate is falsy-or-blank, and only running both lists tells you
  that.

  List 2 has no brand-kit overrides at all. So the exception report has to be
  correct when it's empty. A check that asserts "the exceptions section is
  present and non-empty" fits list 1 and fails list 2 — and a check that only
  counts overrides never notices list 2 has a different problem shape.

  List 2 has no ambiguous domains. This is the one that matters most for the
  identity decision I flagged earlier. Whether I report 209-and-flag or
  merge-to-205, list 2 gives the same answer either way — 99. So list 2 cannot
  validate that choice; it can only prove I didn't hardcode it. The ambiguity is
  load-bearing in one list and absent in the other, which is precisely why the
  two "do not produce the same shape of answer": list 1's report needs an
  ambiguous-needs-a-human section, list 2's is legitimately empty.

  The page-boundary distribution is inverted. In list 1, 5 of 13 duplicate
  groups straddle a page boundary; in list 2, only 1 of 15. Per-page dedupe
  therefore looks much less broken on list 2. Had the starter shipped only list
  2, the page-size drift would be far easier to miss. That inversion looks
  intentional — the two lists stress the same bug at different intensities.

  Name normalization. Build a normalizer against list 2 alone and you'd only
  ever handle the Inc suffix; list 1 also needs case folding (ALDER HEALTH,
  SABLE WORKS). Build it against list 1 and you'd handle both — which is the
  point of having list 1 be the richer one.

  Row id prefixes differ so any code that parses or sorts on row-NNNN breaks.

  What this means for how I build

  The check has to be written against the interface and the invariants, never
  against either file — no hardcoded 209 or 99, no fixture-shaped special cases
  (TASK.md forbids both explicitly). And make verify isn't a second smoke test I
  glance at; it's the thing that catches me fitting to target_accounts.json.

  One caveat worth stating plainly: two hand-built lists are still a weak
  held-out set. Passing both is evidence I didn't fit to one file — it is not
  evidence the fix generalizes to a real upload. The stronger generalization
  argument comes from sources.py: a check that survives all five paging
  pathologies, including correctly refusing to declare completion on
  SilentlyShortLoader, holds for reasons that have nothing to do with either
  fixture. I'd rather lean the "why it holds for the next list" argument on that
  than on list 2.

❯ So one thing I just noticed is that that the readme.md says the user uploaded 
  217 in target_accounts.json. But you are mentioning 209/205. Where is this    
  discrepency coming from. Or am I understand soemthing incorrectly?            

⏺ Good catch, and it's actually two separate discrepancies stacked on top of
  each other. Let me verify the second one.

⏺ Bash(python3 -c "
      import json,re,collections…)
  ⎿  actual rows in file: 223
     README claims      : 217
     … +16 lines (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Bash(python3 -c "
      import json…)
  ⎿  file positions (0-based) of the 2xxx block:
        idx  12  row-2401  Halverson Freight
        idx  37  row-2402  Pell & Sons Ironworks
     … +201 lines (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ You're not misunderstanding anything — you've found a real inconsistency in
  the starter, and it's a different one from the 209/205 question. There are
  three numbers in play, in two different units.

  The units differ

  217 and 223 count rows. 209 and 205 count logical companies. A row is one line
  in the upload; a company can be named by several rows. So 217 vs 209 was
  never an apples-to-apples comparison — but 217 vs 223 is, and that one's a
  genuine bug in the README.

  The README is wrong, and the miss is exactly explainable

  The file has 223 rows. The README says 217. The row ids explain the gap
  precisely:

  id block 1xxx: 217 rows  range row-1001..row-1217
  id block 2xxx:   6 rows  range row-2401..row-2406

  The 2xxx block is exactly the six rows with a null company_id — Halverson 
  Freight, Pell & Sons Ironworks, Cobalt Ridge Dental, Northgate Tutoring,
  Riverbend Cold Storage, Aster Point Realty. Every 1xxx row carries an id; no
  2xxx row does. So the README's 217 is the count of rows the system could 
  resolve to a company, and the six unresolved imports were left out of it.

  They're scattered through the file (positions 12, 37, 61, 98, 149, 202), not
  appended in a block — so this isn't someone forgetting to update a count after
  tacking rows onto the end. Whether it's a stale doc or a planted anchor I
  can't tell, but either way the effect is that the README under-reports the 
  upload by exactly the rows that are hardest to handle. That's the same six
  rows the starter's str(None) bug collapses into one. A reader who trusts the
  README never sees them go missing.

  The ladder from 223 to 209

  223  rows in the file
       ├─ 217  rows carrying a company_id   ──► 203 distinct companies
       │                                         (14 rows are repeat mentions:
       │                                          12 pairs + 1 triple)
       └─   6  rows carrying none           ──►   6 distinct companies
                                                  (unique domains, zero
  collisions)

       203 + 6 = 209 logical companies
       209 − 4 = 205 if you merge the ambiguous same-domain pairs

  So: 223 rows → 209 companies. The 14 dropped are duplicate mentions of a
  company already in the list; nothing is lost. The 4 in the 209/205 gap are the
  -emea subsidiary cases where the upload asserts two ids on one domain and I
  don't think we should silently pick.

  Why this matters beyond a doc typo

  It means no number in the starter is trustworthy without deriving it yourself
  — the README says 217, the trace claims source_row_count: 209, the demo prints
  214 rows campaigned and 204 distinct company ids, and none of them are the
  answer to "how many companies did the customer ask for." I'd treat this as
  evidence for the roadmap rather than a cleanup task: the check has to compute
  the company count from the loaded rows and show its work, so the count is
  reproducible instead of quoted.

  I'd leave the README's 217 alone for now and note it in DECISIONS.md under
  what I found. Changing it is a one-line doc fix, but it's also the clearest
  artifact of the anchoring problem — worth showing a reviewer rather than
  quietly correcting. Your call if you'd rather I fix it and note it.

⏺ The traces are three separate runs interleaved in one file, and I was able to
  reconstruct the main one exactly. This turned out to be the most informative
  thing in the repo.

  t-9f21 — the run that reported success

  Three tool_result events, then the output and the check. The arithmetic
  doesn't work at first glance: 25 + 25 + 17 = 67 rows read, yet it claims
  source_row_count: 209. You can't have 209 companies from 67 rows.

  The resolution is that the log is elided — it records the first two pages and
  the last, not the six in between. Which means the last page's row_count: 17 is
  a fingerprint of the total. I tested it:

  all 223 rows -> 9 pages, last page row_count=23
  only 217     -> 9 pages, last page row_count=17    <-- trace says 17

  So the service served 217 rows, not the 223 in the file. I then ran the
  starter's planner over just those 217 rows:

  all 223 rows          -> source_row_count=214, deliverables=856
  only 1xxx (217 rows)  -> source_row_count=209, deliverables=836
  trace t-9f21 claimed  -> source_row_count=209, deliverable_count=836

  Three independent numbers — last-page count, row count, deliverable count —
  all reproduce exactly on the 217-row hypothesis and none of them reproduce on
  223. That's a confirmed reconstruction, not a guess.

  What that run actually shipped:

  ┌─────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                     │                                                   │
  ├─────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ companies never     │ 6 (the null-company_id rows)                      │
  │ served at all       │                                                   │
  ├─────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ companies shipped   │ 6 — sable-works, kestrel-dynamics, harbor-group,  │
  │ twice               │ ironwood-logistics, vantage-networks,             │
  │                     │ tessellate-energy                                 │
  ├─────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ deliverables in the │ 36 in brand-kit-2019-legacy vs 800 in the         │
  │  wrong brand kit    │ requested Meridian 2026                           │
  ├─────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ what the check said │ passed: true                                      │
  └─────────────────────┴───────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  The 6 doubled companies are exactly the ones whose duplicate rows straddled a
  page boundary at page_size=25 and so escaped the per-page dedupe. The 8
  duplicate groups that happened to land inside one page were collapsed. That's
  the mechanism: which companies get double-billed is a function of page size,
  nothing else.

  The 209 is right by accident — this is the trap

  Worth pausing on. The run claimed 209, and I independently calculated 209
  logical companies. Same number, completely different composition:

  claimed 209 = 203 companies served once  +  6 duplicate rows that survived
    true  209 = 203 companies served once  +  6 companies never served at all

  The six extra shipments and the six missing companies cancel out perfectly. So
  the headline number looks defensible, the shipped check agrees, and the
  customer is still right that something is wrong — they're paying for 6
  duplicates and 6 of their targets got nothing. This is why "the number 
  matches" can never be the completeness test. A check that compares counts
  passes this run. Only a check that matches deliverables back to specific
  uploaded rows catches it.

  t-77b3 — the rerun that never came back

  row_count 25, next_cursor "25", truncated true
  row_count 25, next_cursor "25", truncated true      <-- same cursor
  worker_timeout, elapsed 90s, "No terminal campaign result was recorded."

  The cursor doesn't advance. build_campaign_plan follows next_cursor with no
  progress check, so it spins until the worker is killed. This is StallingLoader
  from sources.py — I reproduced the same hang earlier (RuntimeError: page 
  budget exhausted). It maps directly onto the customer's "I also kicked off a 
  rerun that never came back."

  t-4c08 — the decoy

  An image_search timeout marked retryable: true, and a template_cache cold
  start that rebuilt itself in 310ms. Both self-healing, neither touches counts,
  identity, or brand kits. It's also the one run with no terminal event — no
  output, no evaluation — so its outcome is simply unknown.

  I read this as deliberately planted noise. TASK.md warns "Don't repair 
  behavior you can't tie to the customer's complaint," and this is the thing
  that looks like a failure but isn't one of theirs. I'd name it in DECISIONS.md
  under what I chose not to fix.

  The log itself is a finding

  Pages 3–8 of a 9-page read are missing. The event stream records what the run
  concluded (complete: true, a row count, a deliverable count) but not enough of
  what it did to check that conclusion. Nothing in it mentions brand kits at
  all — the 36 legacy-kit deliverables leave no trace whatsoever. So the audit
  trail can confirm a claim's shape but never its truth, which is the same
  weakness as the shipped check, one layer down.

  Mapping to the customer's note

  ┌────────────────────────────────┬────────────────────────────────────────┐

  ├────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────┤

  Every line in their note is now accounted for, and the six they couldn't have
  known about — the never-served companies — are the most serious.

  One caveat on the reconstruction: it proves the service delivered 217 rows,
  not why. Whether upstream dropped the unresolvable rows before serving them,
  or the fixture represents a later state of the upload, I can't tell from
  what's here. It doesn't change the fix — a reader must reconcile against what
  it was asked to load and refuse to claim completion otherwise — but I'd flag
  it as open rather than assert a cause.

  policy choice, and list 2 can't test it. The load-bearing argument for 209 has
  to be "we don't silently merge what the upload asserts as distinct," not

⏺ Both files are in the folder now (claude_discrepancies.md was moved and
  renamed). They're complementary, not redundant — step1 is a static row
  inventory plus an explicit counting rule; new is provenance inference plus
  scanner output. They agree on every headline number: 209 and 99, 6 and 2

  I checked the numeric claims rather than diffing prose. Four things worth your
  attention — two are errors, two are unreconciled framings.

  Errors

  1. step1 summary table: "13 groups, 28 rows" — it's 27.

  LIST1: groups=13  rows in those groups=27  extra=14   histogram {2:12, 3:1}
  LIST2: groups=15  rows in those groups=31  extra=16   histogram {2:14, 3:1}

  12 pairs + 1 triple = 27, and step1's own prose says "12 pairs + 1 triple."
  List 2's 31 is correct. The downstream numbers are unaffected — extra-rows is
  14 either way, so 203 and 209 still hold. It's a typo in one table cell, but
  it's in the reconciliation table a reviewer will check.

  2. new §2: "The base upload is clean" — it isn't.

  8 of the 9 brand-kit override rows live in row-1001..row-1200, the range that
  section calls clean:

  override rows: row-1014, row-1213, row-1082, row-1084, row-1123,
                 row-1140, row-1163, row-1176, row-1195
  in the "clean" 1001-1200 range: 8 of 9

  The preceding sentence — "Every row that makes the count arguable lives above 
  row-1200" — is true, because overrides don't change the company count. The
  generalization to "clean" is what breaks, and it contradicts that same file's
  own scanner output. Narrow it to "every row that makes the count arguable" and
  it's fine.

  Unreconciled framings

  3. "5 ambiguous" vs "4 same-domain groups." new §10 says "209, of which 6 
  unidentified and 5 ambiguous." step1 §D lists 4 same-domain groups; my
  context.md says 4. All three are right about different things:

  - 4 = same-domain/different-ID groups → the 209-vs-205 gap
  - 5 = ambiguous companies needing human review → 3 -emea + copperline-energy +
  kestrel-robotics

  Different units, same underlying data. Pick one canonical phrasing before
  DECISIONS.md, because a reviewer seeing 4 and 5 in two files will read it as
  an inconsistency.

  4. new §3 demonstrates the both-directions bug only on list 2 — list 1 does it
  too, and worse. I decomposed every page size:

  LIST1 (true=209)                          LIST2 (true=99)
   ps= 10: 214 = 209 +5 dups  -0 deleted     ps= 10: 103 = 99 +4 -0
   ps= 25: 214 = 209 +5 dups  -0 deleted     ps= 25: 100 = 99 +1 -0
   ps=100: 211 = 209 +5 dups  -3 deleted     ps=100:  98 = 99 +0 -1

  At page_size=100, list 1 silently deletes 3 real companies — three of the six
  blank-identity rows land on one page and merge. That's a stronger
  demonstration of §3's point than the list-2 example it uses, and it's on the
  customer's actual list.

  This also fills a gap in step1: it explains list 1's +5 at all three page
  sizes (correct — I verified 5 straddling groups at 10, 25, and 100) but never
  accounts for why ps=100 gives 211 rather than 214. The missing −3 is exactly
  this.

  Claims I verified as correct

  - step1's entire list-2 page analysis — 4 / 1 / 0 straddling groups at ps
  10/25/100, and the blank-row collapse at 100. Exact.
  - step1's "five far-apart groups split at all three page sizes" for list 1.

  SilentlyShortLoader serves: 125 of 223 rows, final truncated=False

  On (1) — you're right, and the assumption is worse than just picking a side.
  The four groups contain 8 company IDs, verified. My "company at risk if

  as "at risk" presented a presentation decision as a fact.

  One correction to your proposed wording: "Four later-added records" holds for
  three of them, not four.

  company-northwind-energy-emea     rows=['row-1213']              above 1200?
  True
  company-sable-fitness-emea        rows=['row-1214']              above 1200?

  True

  False

  company-copperline-energy is a base-block company — row-1100, sitting in the
  supposedly clean range. It isn't later-added at all; a later row (row-1217)
  drags it onto copperline-group.example. So it's a later-added row pulling an
  existing company into conflict, which is a different mechanism from the three
  -emea records.

⏺ Both critiques applied across all three files. make demo, make test, make 
  verify still clean (docs-only changes, but worth confirming).

  (1) The 4-vs-5 wording. Rewritten to your formulation. The canonical section
  in step1_discrepancies.md now leads with "4 contested identity relationships, 
  involving 8 company IDs", and the table lists both IDs per domain with their
  row numbers instead of naming one side. Added explicitly:

  ▎ The count drops by 4 regardless of which side of each pair survives — which 
  ▎ side is absorbed is a policy choice, not a fact in the data. Earlier drafts 
  ▎ of this note listed only the -emea/Energy side as "at risk," which silently 
  ▎ assumed the other side was canonical.

  I kept that self-correction in the file rather than quietly overwriting it,
  since the assumption is exactly the kind of thing the write-up should show
  being caught.

  One adjustment to your wording. "Four later-added records are at risk" holds
  for three of the four. company-copperline-energy is a base-block company at
  row-1100; the later row-1217 pulls it onto copperline-group.example. So all
  three files now distinguish: three -emea records are later-added companies;
  the fourth is a later-added row dragging a pre-existing company into conflict.
  Different mechanism, and it matters because row-1100 sits inside the range §2
  calls clean — the two critiques turn out to touch the same row.

  (2) The 1001-1200 range. Table cell now reads internally unique company IDs 
  and domains; 8 request overrides. The prose uses your framing and adds a
  verified number: 14 of the 200 share a domain with a row above row-1200,
  including row-1020, row-1047, row-1076, row-1093 — the base-block halves of
  the four contested relationships. Closing line is now "internally consistent 
  on identity, not defect-free."

  Terminology swept. new_discrepancies.md §10 and context.md (five spots: the
  units table, the ladder, the list-2 trap, the complaint mapping, the open
  question) all use "relationships." Grep confirms zero remaining uses of
  "identity group", "Company at risk", "no identity defects", or "28 rows". Each
  file states that 4, 8 and 5 answer different questions and tells the reader
  to say which they mean.

  One consequence worth flagging: context.md's open-questions entry now records
  that the merge decision is doubly open — whether to merge, and which ID
  survives if you do. That's a stronger statement of uncertainty than before,
  and I think it's the honest one, but it does mean DECISIONS.md has two choices
  to defend rather than one.

❯ Why not use domain as the identifier, it seems that it is there for all the   
  comapnies                                                                     

⏺ Worth testing rather than arguing. Let me check whether domain is actually
  complete, and what it would cost.

⏺ Bash(python3 -c "
      import json,collections…)
  ⎿  === LIST1 ===
       rows                      : 223
       rows missing a domain     : 0
     … +16 lines (ctrl+o to expand)

  Read 1 file (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Domain is genuinely complete — I checked, and that part of your intuition is
  right:

  LIST1: rows missing a domain 0 | missing a company_id 6
  LIST2: rows missing a domain 0 | missing a company_id 2

  So domain is the more populated field. But completeness isn't what makes a
  good key, and domain fails on the property that matters.

  What domain-as-key actually costs

  LIST1  companies keyed by DOMAIN : 207
         companies under our rule  : 209

    real companies DELETED by domain-merge : 4
    real companies SPLIT   by domain-key   : 2
    209 − 4 + 2 = 207

  It's wrong in both directions at once:

  - Deletes 4. The contested relationships collapse —
  company-northwind-energy-emea, company-sable-fitness-emea,
  company-tessellate-capital-emea and company-copperline-energy vanish into
  their domain-mates. Four companies the customer uploaded receive nothing,
  silently.
  - Splits 2. company-kestrel-robotics carries kestrel-robotics.example and
  kestrel-group.example; company-copperline-energy carries two as well.
  Domain-keying turns each into two companies, so they get billed twice.

  The net (207) is only 2 away from 209, which is what makes this dangerous —
  the total looks close while six individual companies are wrong.

  The trap you'd walk into

  LIST2  companies keyed by DOMAIN : 99
         companies under our rule  : 99

  Identical. List 2 has zero domain collisions, so a domain-keyed implementation
  passes make verify cleanly. You'd ship a fix that looks validated by the
  held-out list and silently destroys four of the customer's actual accounts.
  This is the exact failure the second list exists to catch — except on this
  particular question list 2 is the one that can't catch it, which is why
  new_discrepancies.md §5 calls the -emea rows "the trap that punishes a
  domain-based fix."

  The underlying reason

  company_id is an identity assertion — the upload telling us what it considers
  one customer. domain is an attribute — a personalization input, the thing we
  build the landing page against. Key on an attribute and every attribute error
  becomes an identity error.

  That's not hypothetical here. Both real-world patterns appear in this file:
  subsidiaries sharing a parent's domain (the -emea rows), and one company
  holding several domains after a rebrand or acquisition (kestrel-robotics).
  Neither is a data defect. Domain is simply not unique per company in either
  direction.

  There's also a durability point: domain being 100% present is a property of
  these two fixtures, not a guarantee. Nothing in the interface promises it.
  Whereas missing company_id is a failure mode we've already built for
  explicitly.

  We do use domain — just not as a key

  src/identity.py already encodes this, and it's rule 3:

  ▎ Name and domain are evidence. They are reported as conflicts and never used 
  ▎ to decide identity.

  So domain does real work: it's what surfaces the 4 contested relationships and
  the 2 multi-domain companies for human review. It's the detector, not the
  key. Using it as the key would destroy the very signal that lets us flag them.

  One live question your suggestion does open, though. For the 6 rows with no
  company_id, identity.py rule 2 makes each row its own company keyed by row id
  — it deliberately does not fall back to domain. In these fixtures both rules
  give 6, because all six blank-id rows have distinct domains. So the data does 
  not test that choice. If a future upload had two blank-id rows sharing a
  domain, domain-fallback would merge them and the current rule would not. The
  current rule is the more conservative one — absent identity is treated as
  unresolvable rather than guessed — and I think that's right given the customer
  explicitly can't tell some of these apart. But it's untested by both fixtures
  and belongs in DECISIONS.md as an open item rather than an established
  result.

  ├────────────────────────────┼────────────┼──────────────────┼────────────┤
  │ company-vantage-networks   │ 1127, 1211 │ software         │ —          │
  └────────────────────────────┴────────────┴──────────────────┴────────────┘

  List 2 — 15 duplicated company_ids

  All 15 agree on segment: ambit4 retail, bramble real_estate, cinder3
  hospitality, fennel2 education, garnet4 manufacturing, kestrel2 manufacturing,
  pinnacle2 logistics, quill4 fintech, sable fintech, straddle-works
  manufacturing (×3), talbot3 healthcare, thistle3 media, windrow retail,
  wrenfield2 energy, yarrow4 real_estate. No domain conflicts either.

  What this tells us

  I think you may have expected segments to differ — that would have been a
  warning sign. The opposite result is the useful one: segment is independent 
  corroboration that grouping by company_id is correct. Three attributes could
  have disagreed within a group; here's how they actually behave:

  - segment — agrees in 28 of 28 groups. Never contested.
  - name — differs in 25 of 28, but only cosmetically (Inc suffix, ALL-CAPS). No
  group has a genuinely different company name.
  - domain — agrees in 26 of 28. The two exceptions are company-kestrel-robotics
  and company-copperline-energy.

  So every duplicate group agrees on the two substantive attributes except those
  two rows, and the disagreements that do exist are exactly the ones already
  flagged. Had a group shown, say, company-vantage-capital as both
  transportation and healthcare, that would be real evidence the ID was reused
  for two different customers and merging was wrong. Nothing like that exists.

  This strengthens the 209 defense. The counting rule ("same nonblank company_id
  → one company") isn't just a policy assertion now — the data independently
  supports it on every group, with two named exceptions that we surface rather
  than resolve.

  One caveat: Vantage Capital is segmented transportation, and Alder Health /
  Bright Foods are manufacturing. The segment taxonomy in list 1 is noisy —
  names and segments frequently don't correspond, and the scanner already flags
  mixed conventions (financial services vs real_estate). That noise doesn't
  affect this result, since we're comparing segment within a group rather than
  judging whether it's plausible, but it means segment is weak evidence in the
  other direction — I wouldn't use a segment match to justify merging anything
  company_id didn't already join.

  Want me to add a segment-conflict check to the audit? It reports zero on both
  fixtures, which is precisely why it's worth having — it's a guard for the next
  list, and a check that's silent here proves it isn't fitted to these files.
  I'd add it as a general "conflicting attributes within one identity" class
  alongside the existing domain check rather than a segment-specific rule.

⏺ I verified the technical claims. Both alleged code bugs reproduce, and most of
  the corrections are right. Here's where I land.

  The two code bugs are real — and one is serious

  Claim 4 (fingerprint data loss) is the most important thing in these notes.

  upload: 50 rows, second half byte-identical to first half
  rows collected: 25 of 50   complete=True
  reason: service reported the end of the list

  collect_rows silently discarded half the upload and declared success. That is
  the same defect class as SilentlyShortLoader — a short read claiming
  completion — except we'd be introducing it ourselves while claiming to fix it.
  Worse, paging.py:47 makes the choice deliberately:

  ▎ "Deliberately not keyed on row ids: an upload is not guaranteed to give 
  ▎ every row a unique one."

  The docstring reasons its way into the bug. And it contradicts itself: if row
  ids aren't unique, then two legitimate pages can be byte-identical, which is
  exactly the case that breaks. Not triggered by either fixture, but it's a
  P0-severity latent defect in code the plan proposed shipping. The suggested
  fix — consider cursor transitions, and mark unverified rather than silently
  discard when replay can't be distinguished — is right.

  Claim 3 (blank identity collision) also reproduces:

  keys: ['unidentified:dup-1', 'unidentified:dup-1']
  companies produced: 1  -> BUG: MERGED

  Two distinct companies, both blank identity, same uploaded id → merged. That
  directly contradicts identity.py's own rule 2. Keying on index rather than the
  row label fixes it.

  Both of these are in code I reviewed and endorsed. I missed them.

  My factual errors — all confirmed

  C2's numbers were wrong, and the correction is better than what I wrote:

  list1 ps=25  referenced=214 of 223 -> unreferenced=9
  list1 ps=100 referenced=211 of 223 -> unreferenced=12
  list2 ps=25  referenced=100 of 115 -> unreferenced=15

  I wrote "14 and 16" — those are the post-fix absorbed counts, not the current
  state. The reframing is correct and sharper: C2 isn't an existing defect, it's
  one that B1/B3 would introduce if plural provenance didn't ship in the same
  change. That makes it a co-requisite of step 1, not a step-3 nicety. The
  page-size dependence (9 vs 12) is B1 leaking into provenance, which I hadn't
  noticed.

  B2's "5 lost" — at ps=100 it's 3, not 5. My own earlier measurement said blank
  rows=6 on 3 pages -> 3 companies DELETED and I wrote the wrong number into
  the plan. "Up to 5; 3 at ps=100" is right.

  Issue count — I wrote "fourteen" and listed sixteen (3+3+3+3+2+2). My error.

  The pathspec doesn't reproduce — confirmed, Makefile is excluded so that line
  can't appear.

  Steps 1 and 2 aren't separable — correct. Grouping after the full read
  requires the full read, which is collect_rows.

  Where I'd push back

  On #1 (brand kit) — I concede the main point but not the framing.

  You're right and I was wrong. My reasoning was that we can tie "it happened
  silently" to the complaint but not "it exists at all." But the customer said
  "some of the creative is not in the brand we picked" — they're complaining
  about the creative being wrong, not about not being told. Request-wins is the
  correct default.

  Where I'd differ: the three proposed remedies aren't parallel. If the request 
  wins, there's no violation left to block or mark incomplete. Blocking or
  complete=False are only needed if you keep row-overrides-win. So I'd take
  request-wins as the single resolution, keep the override recorded as
  reportable data (9 rows wanted a different kit; request took precedence;
  review if intentional), and drop the block/incomplete branch. Deleting the
  mechanism entirely still overreaches — a per-account kit may be a real feature
  — but it must never beat an explicit request.

  On the t-9f21 reconstruction — partial disagreement. Two claims are being
  collapsed:

  - The mechanism is reproducible, not inferred. Running the starter over the
  217 1xxx rows produces 209 and 836 exactly, and 214/856 over all 223. Anyone
  can re-run it. The six-cancels-six decomposition follows from that.
  - The provenance is inference — that the log is elided, and why the service
  served 217. I already flagged the "why" as open in context.md.

  So "plausible but not proven" undersells it, but my "confirmed reconstruction,
  not a guess" oversold it. The honest label is inference with three-way 
  numeric corroboration (last-page 17, rows 209, deliverables 836 — all matching
  217, none matching 223), with the reproduction command shown.

  Where the notes are right and I'd just adopt them

  - #2 evaluator contract — my four conditions were too loose. "Every company
  has 4 assets" doesn't say which four; brand/template conformity, no orphans,
  no duplicates, correct attribution all belong as explicit obligations.
  - #5 conflicting domains — real gap. The plan flags kestrel-robotics and
  copperline-energy but never says which domain enters the deliverable. Picking
  the first is the ordering accident I criticized elsewhere.
  - #6 complete=True (unverified) — agreed, and my plan was internally
  inconsistent: A3 proposes the claimed/verified split, then the headline table
  prints complete flag: True → True. A boolean can't carry the qualifier.
  - #5 in the second block — A3 is more closable than I claimed. This is the one
  where I was too pessimistic. demo.py already has len(accounts) and simply
  doesn't pass it. An upload record declaring its own size is independent of the
  paging service — that's not reading behind the interface, it's how a real
  upload works. A3 belongs in "partly closed," not Known Limits.
  - Unidentified companies × 4 — correct, 836 = 209 × 4 silently decides that
  companies we cannot identify still get personalized creative. That's a real
  choice and it should be stated, not inferred from arithmetic.
  - C2/E2 severity and the adversarial tests — both right.

  Net

  Sixteen corrections, I'd act on fifteen. The two reproduced code bugs change
  the plan's shape most: paging.py needs work before it's wired in, not after.
  Say the word and I'll revise FIX_PLAN.md — including renumbering to 16 issues,
  fixing the C2/B2 numbers, merging steps 1–2, moving A3 to partly-closed, and
  adding the fingerprint and blank-id-collision defects as new P0 items.

      137 +### A4. Page fingerprinting discards legitimate duplicate page
          +s — **P0, NEW**                                               
      138  
      139 +**Problem.** `paging.py:47` fingerprints a page by its seriali
          +sed content and                                               
      140 +drops any page whose fingerprint was seen before. Two *legitim
          +ate* pages with                                               
      141 +identical row content are therefore treated as a replay and si
          +lently discarded.                                             
      142 +                                                              
      143 +**Reproduced:**                                               
      144 +                                                              
      145 +```                                                           
      146 +upload: 50 rows, second half byte-identical to first half     
      147 +rows collected: 25 of 50   complete=True                      
      148 +reason: service reported the end of the list                  
      149 +```                                                           
      150 +                                                              
      151 +**Half the upload lost, and the reader declared success.** Thi
          +s is the same                                                 
      152 +defect class as `SilentlyShortLoader` (A3) — except introduced
          + by our own fix,                                              
      153 +in code this plan previously proposed shipping unchanged.     
      154 +                                                              
      155 +The docstring reasons its way into the bug and contradicts its
          +elf:                                                          
      156 +                                                              
      157 +> *"Deliberately not keyed on row ids: an upload is not guaran
          +teed to give every                                            
      158 +> row a unique one, and a replayed page is a property of the p
          +age."*                                                        
      159 +                                                              
      160 +If row ids are not guaranteed unique, then two legitimate page
          +s **can** be                                                  
      161 +byte-identical — which is exactly the failing case. Not trigge
          +red by either                                                 
      162 +fixture, so no existing test would catch it.                  
      163 +                                                              
      164 +**Solution.** Replay detection must consider the **cursor tran
          +sition**, not page                                            
      165 +content alone. A page is a replay only if the cursor did not a
          +dvance. Where a                                               
      166 +replay genuinely cannot be distinguished from legitimate dupli
          +cate content, mark                                            
      167 +the result **unverified** rather than silently discarding rows
          + — never lose data                                            
      168 +to a heuristic.                                               
      169 +                                                              
      170 +**Expected:** the 50-row case returns `rows=50, complete=True`
          +;                                                             
      171 +`ReplayingLoader` still returns `rows=223, complete=True`. Bot
          +h become tests.                                               
      172 +                                                              
      173  ---

⏺ Revised. 19 issues now (was 16, miscounted as 14), including three new ones.

  What changed

  Three new issues, two of them reproduced bugs in code the old plan proposed 
  shipping:

  - A4 (P0) — paging.py fingerprints pages by content, so two legitimate
  identical pages are treated as a replay. Reproduced: 25 of 50 rows collected,
  complete=True. Same defect class as SilentlyShortLoader, introduced by our own
  fix. The docstring reasons its way into it and contradicts itself — it says
  row ids aren't guaranteed unique, which is exactly what makes two legit pages
  byte-identical.
  - B4 (P0) — blank-identity rows keyed on the uploaded id collide. Reproduced:
  two distinct companies → one. Contradicts identity.py's own rule 2.
  - C4 (P1) — no rule for which domain personalizes kestrel-robotics /
  copperline-energy.

  C3 reversed. The request now wins. Your argument was right — the complaint is
  that the creative is wrong, not that they weren't told. List 1 goes to 836 in 
  the requested kit, 0 legacy, with the 9 rows recorded as exceptions rather
  than silently applied. One thing I'd note: the three remedies floated aren't
  parallel — once the request wins there's no violation left to block or mark
  incomplete, so I dropped that branch.

  A3 moved to partly-closable. You were right that I was too pessimistic.
  demo.py already holds len(accounts) and just doesn't pass it; an upload
  declaring its own size is independent of the paging service. Residual limit is
  now "a caller with no declared count," not "undetectable."

  D1 expanded from 4 conditions to 9 — asset exclusivity (four landing pages
  must fail), no orphans, no duplicates, correct attribution, request
  conformity. D2 replaces the boolean with service_claimed_complete /
  verified_complete: true|false|unknown / completion_reason.

  Corrections applied: C2's numbers (9/12/15/17, reframed as a co-requisite of
  step 1 rather than an existing defect); B2's "up to 5; 3 at ps=100"; steps 1–2
  merged; pathspec now includes Makefile; C2→P1 and E2→P1; test plan 8 → 14
  including the six adversarial cases.

  Two places I didn't simply adopt

  The t-9f21 label. I split it rather than calling the whole thing an inference.
  The mechanism is reproducible — anyone can re-run the starter over the 217
  rows and get 209/836. What's inference is that the log is elided and why 217
  was served. The plan now says "inference with three-way numeric
  corroboration," which I think is the accurate middle.

  C4 is left as your decision, not mine. Withholding gives 828 deliverables + 2 
  blocked; deterministic-pick-and-flag gives 836 + 2 flagged. I recommend
  withholding — it's consistent with reporting 209 and flagging rather than
  merging to 205 — but it moves the headline number, so it's flagged as an open
  decision before step 3.

  Known limit #4 is new and worth reading: A4 and B4 were found by inspection, 
  and neither fixture triggers them. There's no reason to think inspection found
  the last such case.

⏺ I tested my own claim and it partly broke. Worth leading with that.

  1. The t-9f21 reconstruction — weaker than I said

  I claimed "three independent numbers all match." I checked both halves of
  that:

  836 = 209 x 4 -> True   (derived from 209, not independent evidence)
  last page 17  -> 217 rows served  (217 mod 25 = 17 — ANY 217-row set gives 17)

  So it was never three numbers. 836 carries no information beyond 209, and 17
  pins the count of rows served without saying anything about which rows. That
  leaves two real constraints, not three.

  Then I asked how special the row-2xxx hypothesis actually is — if you drop any
  6 rows at random, how often do you land on 209?

  row-2xxx hypothesis -> 209   (trace claims 209)
  random 6-row drops  -> 1418/3000 produce 209  (47.3%)
  outcomes: 209:1418  208:919  210:566  211:88  212:9

  209 is the single most likely result of dropping any 6 rows. The numeric match
  is close to worthless as evidence for which six. I had presented a 47%-likely
  coincidence as confirmation.

  What actually survives

  ┌─────────────────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────┬──────────┐
  │              Claim              │           Support           │ Strength │
  ├─────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────┼──────────┤
  │ The log is elided (3 of 9 pages │ Forced — 67 rows cannot     │ Entailed │
  │  recorded)                      │ yield 209 companies         │          │
  ├─────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────┼──────────┤
  │ The service served 217 rows,    │ Last page = 17 at ps=25     │ Strong   │
  │ not 223                         │                             │          │
  ├─────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────┼──────────┤
  │ The six missing were the        │ Structural, not numeric     │ Moderate │
  │ blank-company_id rows           │                             │          │
  └─────────────────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────┴──────────┘

  The last row is the one I overstated. Its real support has nothing to do with
  209:

  - README.md independently says 217 — a separate document, written before any
  of this analysis, agreeing on the count.
  - row-1001..row-1217 is exactly 217 rows and row-2401..row-2406 is exactly 6 —
  two clean contiguous id blocks.
  - Every 1xxx row has a company_id; no 2xxx row does. The split is perfectly
  aligned with the identity boundary.

  That's a decent argument from structure and corroborating documentation. It is
  not the argument I made. And it's abductive — best available explanation, not
  proof.

  Nothing downstream changes. The fix is the same either way: reconcile against
  what you were asked to load, and refuse to claim completion otherwise. What
  changes is how hard I'm allowed to lean on it in the write-up. context.md
  currently says "a confirmed reconstruction, not a guess" and FIX_PLAN.md says
  "three-way numeric corroboration" — both are now wrong and I should correct
  them to something like "217 rows served is well supported; which 217 rests on 
  the id-block structure and the README, not on the 209 match." Say the word and
  I'll fix both files.

  This is also the kind of thing worth putting in SUBMISSION.md under the "claim
  you're least sure of, and how you checked it" — the check is a 15-line script
  and it changed my own conclusion.

  2. Why I left C4 to you

  Not deference for its own sake. Three specific reasons.

  It moves the number the customer reads. 836 versus 828 deliverables, with 2
  companies receiving nothing under withhold. Every other item in the plan is
  code correctness with one right answer; this one trades customer-visible
  output against confidence, and that's a product call.

  My usual tiebreaker points both ways here. Everywhere else I've argued "be
  conservative, don't decide for the customer" — report 209 rather than merging
  to 205, keep blank-identity rows separate rather than folding them. But
  conservatism is directional, and the two cases pull opposite ways:

  ┌───────────────────────────┬─────────────────────┬──────────────────────┐
  │                           │ Conservative choice │        Effect        │
  ├───────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼──────────────────────┤
  │ Identity (209 vs 205)     │ don't merge         │ preserves companies  │
  ├───────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼──────────────────────┤
  │ Domain (kestrel-robotics) │ withhold            │ removes deliverables │
  └───────────────────────────┴─────────────────────┴──────────────────────┘

  So "be conservative" doesn't settle it. The analogy I leaned on for 209
  doesn't transfer cleanly, and I noticed that while writing the revision —
  which is why I flagged rather than picked.

  There's a third option I should have listed. Ship all 836, but emit the
  deliverable with domain marked unresolved and both candidates recorded,
  letting the downstream generator decide or fail. That's neither withhold nor
  guess — it pushes the choice to where the creative is actually produced. It
  fits the "make it traceable" principle better than either option in the plan.
  I left it out because it assumes something about a generator we don't have in
  this repo, but it's arguably the most honest of the three.

  What I'd choose if you want a recommendation: withhold and block. Only 2
  companies out of 209, the customer explicitly said they can't tell some
  entries apart, and a blocked company is visible and reversible in a way that
  wrong creative isn't. But I'd hold that position weakly — the number moves,
  and defending it is the part of the exercise that's yours.

⏺ Good — and kestrel-robotics is a different kind of problem from the EMEA one.
  That distinction matters, and my plan blurred it.

  The data

  company-kestrel-robotics
     row-1066  Kestrel Robotics   logistics   kestrel-robotics.example
     row-1216  Kestrel Robotics   logistics   kestrel-group.example

  Same company_id, same name, same segment. Only the domain differs.

  It is not part of the 209-vs-205 decision

  This is the thing worth being clear on. The two cases are structural inverses:

  ┌─────────────────────┬────────────────┬─────────────────────┬───────────┐
  │                     │     Shape      │      Question       │ Affects   │
  │                     │                │                     │  count?   │
  ├─────────────────────┼────────────────┼─────────────────────┼───────────┤
  │ EMEA / contested    │ 2 company_ids  │ Are these two       │ Yes — 209 │
  │ relationships       │ → 1 domain     │ companies or one?   │  vs 205   │
  ├─────────────────────┼────────────────┼─────────────────────┼───────────┤
  │                     │ 1 company_id → │ Which domain        │           │
  │ kestrel-robotics    │  2 domains     │ personalizes this   │ No        │
  │                     │                │ one company?        │           │
  └─────────────────────┴────────────────┴─────────────────────┴───────────┘

  Your decision not to merge already settles kestrel-robotics' identity —
  company_id is authoritative, so it's one company, and the count stays 209
  regardless. Nothing here is in tension with what you just decided.

  What's unresolved is narrower: this one company has two websites on file, and
  a landing page needs exactly one. That's an output question, not a counting
  question.

  How it differs from copperline-energy

  I lumped these together as "2 companies with conflicting domains." They're not
  equivalent:

  kestrel-robotics.example    rows=1  claimed by ['company-kestrel-robotics']
  kestrel-group.example       rows=1  claimed by ['company-kestrel-robotics']

  copperline-energy.example   rows=1  claimed by ['company-copperline-energy']
  copperline-group.example    rows=3  claimed by ['company-copperline-energy',
                                                   'company-copperline-group']

  Kestrel's conflict is self-contained. Both domains belong exclusively to it.
  Nobody else claims either. It's one company that appears twice with two of its
  own addresses.

  Copperline's conflict is entangled. copperline-group.example is another 
  company's primary domain. Personalizing Copperline Energy's landing page with
  copperline-group.example would put a different company's website on it. That's
  a genuine error, not an ambiguity.

  That difference suggests a general rule that resolves one of them for free:

  ▎ A domain claimed by more than one company_id cannot be used to personalize 
  ▎ any of them.

  Apply it and copperline-energy resolves cleanly to copperline-energy.example —
  the group domain is disqualified because company-copperline-group also claims
  it. Kestrel is untouched, because neither of its domains is contested by
  anyone else. So the rule handles the entangled case on principle, and leaves
  only kestrel genuinely open.

  One pattern, offered with a caveat

  Both follow the same shape, and the rows sit adjacent in the file:

  row-1066  file index  73   base block (1001-1200)
  row-1216  file index  74   re-import block (1201-1217)

  row-1100  file index 114   base block
  row-1217  file index 115   re-import block

  In both, the base row carries the name-matching specific domain and the later 
  re-import row carries a group-level domain. A plausible story is that a
  re-import attached the parent's web domain.

  I'd be careful with this. It's a pattern across two examples in one fixture,
  and "prefer the earliest row" is precisely the ordering accident I've
  criticized elsewhere — it's only defensible if row order reliably reflects
  import order, which nothing guarantees for the next upload. I'd treat it as
  supporting evidence for a human reviewing the flag, not as an automatic rule.

  So what to do with kestrel-robotics

  Three options, now that copperline is handled separately:

  ┌──────────────────┬───────────────────────────┬─────────────────────────┐
  │      Option      │          Result           │        Argument         │
  ├──────────────────┼───────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │                  │                           │ Consistent, but heavy — │
  │ Withhold and     │ 1 company gets nothing    │  nobody disputes it's   │
  │ block            │                           │ one company, and both   │
  │                  │                           │ domains are its own     │
  ├──────────────────┼───────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │ Emit with domain │ Ships, generator decides  │ Most traceable; pushes  │
  │  unresolved,     │ or fails                  │ the choice where        │
  │ both recorded    │                           │ creative is made        │
  ├──────────────────┼───────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │ Pick earliest +  │ Ships with                │ Practical, but we       │
  │ flag             │ kestrel-robotics.example  │ picked, on a heuristic  │
  └──────────────────┴───────────────────────────┴─────────────────────────┘

  I've changed my recommendation. In the plan I said withhold both. Having
  separated them, withholding kestrel looks disproportionate: it's one company,
  both domains are its own, and there's no cross-company contamination risk. I'd
  now say emit with the domain marked unresolved and both candidates recorded,
  and flag it for review — while copperline-energy is resolved by the
  multi-claim rule above rather than by judgment.

  That changes the numbers from what the plan says: 836 deliverables, 0 blocked,
  1 company flagged for domain review — instead of 828 with 2 blocked.

  Two things I'd still want you to weigh: whether "unresolved domain" is
  acceptable output in a system where a landing page must have some URL, and
  whether the multi-claim rule feels like a principled disqualification or a
  convenient one. Both are defensible; the second is the one a reviewer might
  push on.

⏺ Agreed on the count — and that part was never actually at risk. company_id is
  authoritative, so kestrel-robotics is one company and the total stays 209
  regardless of what we do about the domain. Your decision doesn't change any
  number.

  What remains isn't a counting question, it's unavoidable plumbing: a landing
  page has to point at some URL, and there are two on file. We can't not-decide
  — we can only decide well or badly.

  Your read is right, and here's the reason it's right

  The reason "probably just messed up" is safe here is blast radius:

  kestrel-group.example      claimed by ['company-kestrel-robotics']
   <- only Kestrel
  copperline-group.example   claimed by ['company-copperline-energy',
                                          'company-copperline-group']
   <- two companies

  Both of Kestrel's candidate domains are Kestrel's own. Pick the wrong one and
  the landing page points at a different page of the same company's web
  presence. Annoying, not harmful.

  Copperline is not like that. Its second domain is another company's primary
  domain. Pick wrong there and Copperline Energy's landing page shows Copperline
  Group's website — wrong company entirely.

  So the two cases deserve different treatment, and the distinction is
  mechanical rather than a judgment call:

  ▎ A domain claimed by more than one company_id is disqualified for all of 
  ▎ them.

  That resolves copperline-energy to copperline-energy.example on principle, and
  leaves kestrel-robotics alone — because neither of its domains is contested
  by anyone else. Exactly the split you're describing.

  What I'd do for kestrel

  Take the first occurrence in service order and record both. That gives
  kestrel-robotics.example (row-1066, file index 73, base block) over
  kestrel-group.example (row-1216, index 74, re-import block). It's
  deterministic and page-size independent, since collected row order is service
  order, not page order.

  Worth noting the two rules agree where they overlap: first-occurrence also
  picks copperline-energy.example for copperline. That's a small consistency
  check in their favour rather than two rules pulling apart.

  Resulting numbers: list 1 → 836 deliverables, 0 blocked, 1 company flagged as
  a low-severity note. List 2 unchanged at 396. That's simpler than what the
  plan currently says (828 with 2 blocked), and I think it's better.

  The one caveat

  "Same id, same name, same segment — don't care" is right for this row pair.
  I'd avoid encoding it as "domain conflicts never matter." The dangerous
  version of this defect looks identical on those three fields and still ships a
  competitor's URL — that's copperline, where name and segment also match
  exactly. The multi-claim disqualifier is what separates them, so I'd keep it
  as the guard and treat the leftover single-owner conflicts as notes rather
  than blockers.

  Concretely, C4 becomes: disqualify multi-claimed domains → if one candidate
  remains, use it silently → if several remain, take first occurrence and flag →
  never block. Want me to update FIX_PLAN.md to that, along with the C4 numbers
  and the corrected t-9f21 wording we discussed?

⏺ 19 issues confirmed. FIX_PLAN.md is now at revision 3.

  What changed

  C4 is decided and rewritten as an ordered policy rather than a choice between
  options:

  1. Disqualify any domain claimed by more than one company_id
  2. If one candidate remains → use it, no flag
  3. If several remain → first occurrence in service order, report as a review
  note
  4. Never block

  Applied to the fixtures:

  ┌───────────────────┬──────┬───────────────────────────┬──────────────────┐

✻ Worked for 2m 9s

❯ Can you look at thsis and see if we should make the fix:                      
    How a system ends up doing that                                             
                                                                                
    The realistic mechanisms all put a filter upstream of pagination:           
                                                                                
    - An INNER JOIN where a LEFT JOIN was intended. Upload rows live in         
  staging; serving joins them to resolved company records to                    
    populate company_id and company_name. Rows whose identity resolution failed 
  have no company record, so the join silently discards                         
    them. This is the single most common way this happens.                      
    - A "resolved rows only" serving view. Two-phase import: upload → stage 223 
  → enrichment matches domain/name against the account                          
    graph → 6 fail to resolve → the view the campaign reads is WHERE company_id 
  IS NOT NULL. The staging table still holds 223;                               
    nothing reads it.                                                           
    - Keyset pagination on a nullable column. If the cursor is WHERE company_id 
  > :cursor ORDER BY company_id, NULL never satisfies                           
    the predicate — SQL NULL comparisons are never true. Those rows become      
  permanently unreachable, and the scan terminates normally                     
    without them.                                                               
    - A partial index (... WHERE company_id IS NOT NULL) used to order the      
  scan, so an index-only plan never visits the NULL rows.                       
                                                                                
    All four produce identical symptoms: a correct row count, a correct cursor  
  chain, a legitimate partial final page, and a quiet                           
    truncated: false.                                                           
                                                                                
    Why this is the worst version of the bug                                    
                                                                                
    The loss isn't random — it's correlated with difficulty. The rows that      
  vanished are precisely the ones whose identity couldn't be                    
    resolved: the hardest cases, the ones most needing human review. A system   
  with this bug systematically discards its own                                 
    unresolved work and reports success. The harder a row is, the more likely   
  it disappears, and the cleaner the run looks.                                 
                                                                                
    That's also why the customer noticed duplicates but not omissions.          
  Duplicates are visible when you spot-check twenty rows. Six                   
    companies that were never mentioned are invisible by construction — you     
  can't spot-check for absence.                                                 
                                                                                
    What this means for A3 in the fix plan                                      
                                                                                
    A3 is currently framed as "a short read that claims success." That's        
  mis-attributed. The recorded incident was a filtered read                     
    that correctly claimed success, and no amount of hardening in paging.py can 
  see it — the protocol was obeyed exactly.                                     
    SilentlyShortLoader truncates at an offset and isn't a faithful model of    
  what actually happened.                                                       
                                                                                
    The fix doesn't change: reconciling against a declared row count (223 ≠     
  217) catches both. But the framing should, because the                        
    difference tells you where to look — not at the paging service, at whatever 
  assembles the row set before it. Worth adding a sixth                         
    loader that drops rows by predicate rather than by offset, since none of    
  the five in sources.py models this.                                           
                                                                                
    Calibration: this is inference from an exact match on two independent       
  numbers plus the page shape, which is strong but not proof.                   
    The alternative — the six rows were added to the fixture after the run —    
  can't be excluded from the evidence here, though it                           
    requires a re-export that interleaved them rather than appending. Either    
  way, both readings are indistinguishable to any reader                        
    that doesn't reconcile against the upload, which is the actual point.       

⏺ Let me test the page-shape claim, because if it holds it's stronger evidence
  than the analysis states.

⏺ Bash(cd /Users/jaina/Downloads/01-campaign-audit && PYTHONPATH=src python3 -
      <<'EOF'
      import json…)
  ⎿  trace t-9f21 (ps=25): 9 pages implied, final page row_count=17, trunca
     ted=False
     … +11 lines (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Yes — make it. The analysis is right, and the page-shape evidence is stronger 
  than it claims:

  trace t-9f21 (ps=25):        9 pages, final page 17 rows, truncated=False

  SilentlyShortLoader / 223:   5 pages, final page 25 rows, truncated=False
  filtered 217, read to end:   9 pages, final page 17 rows, truncated=False   <-
  exact match
  unfiltered 223:              9 pages, final page 23 rows, truncated=False

  The short-read model isn't merely "not faithful" — it's positively excluded.
  It produces 5 pages with a full 25-row final page; the trace shows 9 pages
  ending in a partial 17. A partial final page is the signature of reading a row
  set to its natural end. So the trace is evidence against truncation and for a
  smaller row set, which is a sharper claim than the note makes.

  That also explains why the two are differently detectable. A short read leaves
  a weak tell (a full final page with truncated: false); a filtered read leaves
  none at all — nine pages, clean cursor chain, natural partial ending, honest
  truncated: false. It is a protocol-perfect read of the wrong row set. Strictly
  the harder bug.

  Three refinements before it goes in

  1. Split A3, don't reframe it. SilentlyShortLoader is still in sources.py and
  still has to be handled — A3 is a correct description of that shape. What's
  mis-attributed is using A3 to explain the customer's incident. So keep A3 and
  add A5: filtered read — a row set that is complete-as-served and
  incomplete-as-uploaded — then repoint the t-9f21 narrative at A5. Reframing A3
  would lose coverage of a shape that's actually in the repo.

  2. Put the sixth loader in tests/, not sources.py. That file documents shapes
  "recorded during past incidents" and "kept because they are the shapes that 
  broke something once." Adding a fabricated one misrepresents its provenance.
  TASK.md explicitly sanctions the alternative: "Your own checks may supply 
  their own implementation of that interface." A FilteringLoader test double
  that drops rows by predicate is cleaner and doesn't put invented history into
  a file of real history.

  3. Narrow the mechanism list — one of the four doesn't fit. Keyset pagination
  on a nullable column (WHERE company_id > :cursor) would emit cursors that look
  like company_ids. The trace shows "25", "50" — numeric offsets. So:

  ┌──────────────────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────┐
  │              Mechanism               │ Consistent with observed cursors? │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────┤
  │ INNER JOIN where LEFT was intended   │ yes                               │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────┤
  │ resolved-rows-only serving view      │ yes                               │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────┤
  │ keyset pagination on nullable column │ no — wrong cursor shape           │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────┤
  │ partial index excluding NULLs        │ agnostic                          │
  └──────────────────────────────────────┴───────────────────────────────────┘

  Worth keeping the keyset case as a general hazard, but not offering it as an
  explanation of this incident.

  What I'd keep exactly as written

  The correlated-with-difficulty argument is the best thing in the note, and it
  belongs in the write-up rather than buried in the plan. The rows that vanished
  are precisely the ones whose identity resolution failed — the system discards
  its own hardest cases and reports success, and the harder a row is, the
  cleaner the run looks.

  It also explains a detail of the customer report I hadn't connected. They
  found duplicates, wrong-brand creative, and ambiguous entries — every one of
  those is visible in what was delivered. They reported no missing companies,
  because absence isn't spot-checkable. Six companies that were never mentioned
  leave no trace in a sample of twenty.

  The calibration paragraph is right and should stay, including the alternative
  it can't exclude (the six rows added to the fixture after the run). That's
  consistent with the 47%-random-drop result — the numbers pin how many rows
  were served, never which. And its closing point is the one that matters: both
  readings are indistinguishable to any reader that doesn't reconcile against
  the upload.

  What doesn't change

  The fix. Reconciling against a declared count catches filtered and short reads
  identically — 223 ≠ 217 either way. What changes is where you'd look
  (upstream of pagination, not at the paging service) and what you'd test
  (drop-by-predicate, which none of the five existing loaders models).

  Want me to apply this to FIX_PLAN.md — add A5, repoint the t-9f21 narrative,
  add the FilteringLoader test, and narrow the mechanism list?
